## Layer 0 — Energy & Power Infrastructure


### 1. Crusoe Energy

*Source: Crusoe_Energy.json*


#### Identity

**Company Name**: Crusoe Energy Systems

**Headquarters**: Denver, Colorado, US

**Headquarters Region**: US

**Founded Year**: 2018


#### Value Chain

**Role In Value Chain**: energy_infrastructure

**Value Chain Position**:
Crusoe sits at the intersection of energy sourcing and cloud compute. It depends on stranded natural gas and renewable energy sources upstream, and serves AI/ML workload customers (enterprises, AI labs like OpenAI, hyperscalers like Oracle) downstream through its Crusoe Cloud platform. It is a key infrastructure partner in the Stargate project (OpenAI/Oracle).

**Vertical Integration Level**: Full-stack


#### Products

**Key Products**:
- Crusoe Cloud — GPU cloud platform (NVIDIA and AMD GPUs) purpose-built for AI/ML workloads with on-demand, spot, and reserved pricing
- AI Data Center Campuses — large-scale facilities (1.2 GW Abilene TX, 1.8 GW Wyoming) co-located with energy sources, including the Stargate campus for OpenAI
- Crusoe Intelligence Foundry — platform for deploying and serving AI models with API access


#### Business

**Revenue Or Valuation**: Valuation above $10B (Series E, October 2025). Revenue: ~$276M in 2024, projected ~$1B in 2025 and ~$2B in 2026. Source: Crusoe press release and Sacra.

**Funding Total And Last Round**:
Total raised: ~$3.9B. Last round: $1.375B Series E at >$10B valuation (October 2025), co-led by Mubadala Capital and Valor Equity Partners. Investors include NVIDIA, Founders Fund, Fidelity, T. Rowe Price, Tiger Global, Salesforce Ventures, Franklin Templeton, Lowercarbon Capital.

**Business Model**:
Usage-based cloud compute (GPU-hours), long-term reserved capacity contracts, and infrastructure-as-a-service for hyperscalers. Revenue split between direct Crusoe Cloud customers and large-scale data center leasing/build-to-suit (e.g., Stargate project with OpenAI/Oracle). Also secured a $750M credit facility from Brookfield for infrastructure buildout.


#### Openness

**Open Vs Closed Spectrum**:
Hybrid — Closed proprietary cloud platform, but maintains open-source tooling on GitHub (Terraform providers, Kubernetes autoscalers, CSI drivers) and supports deployment of open-weight models via Intelligence Foundry.


#### Startup Relevance

**Relevance For Startups**:
Crusoe matters for AI entrepreneurs because it offers a credible alternative to AWS/GCP/Azure for GPU compute at significantly lower cost (claims up to 81% cheaper GPU performance and spot pricing up to 90% off hyperscaler rates). Its vertically integrated energy-to-compute model means structurally lower operating costs, which it passes on to customers. For startups training or serving AI models, Crusoe Cloud provides access to NVIDIA H100/GB200 GPUs with competitive pricing, enterprise-grade uptime (99.98%), and a sustainability story (clean energy powered) that can matter for ESG-conscious investors or European market entry. The company's role in the Stargate project signals it is trusted by the largest AI players, adding credibility.

**Accessibility**:
Accessible to startups via Crusoe Cloud with self-service sign-up, on-demand and spot GPU instances, and API access. Pricing is competitive vs. hyperscalers. No minimum commitment required for on-demand. Reserved instances available for 6-month to 3-year terms at deeper discounts. Early-access programs available with dedicated support.

**Primary Model Dependency**: None — Crusoe is infrastructure-layer and model-agnostic. Supports NVIDIA and AMD GPU workloads.


#### Ecosystem

**Key Partnerships**:
- OpenAI / Oracle — Stargate project, flagship 1.2 GW Abilene TX campus running on Oracle Cloud Infrastructure
- NVIDIA — strategic investor, full-stack GPU solutions (H100, GB200 NVL72), early access to new hardware
- Brookfield — $750M credit facility for infrastructure expansion
- AMD — GPU supply diversification
- Redwood Materials — second-life battery microgrids for energy storage

**Competitive Moat**:
Vertical integration from energy sourcing to cloud delivery. Crusoe owns or controls its energy supply (stranded natural gas, renewables), co-locates data centers at energy sources, and operates its own cloud platform. Energy represents ~60% of AI data center OpEx, and Crusoe's structural cost advantage (up to 1/13th standard energy costs) creates pricing power that pure-play cloud competitors cannot replicate. Additionally, its in-house manufacturing arm accelerates deployment timelines. Power pipeline exceeds 45 GW.

**Data Moat Type**: None


#### Regulatory

**Regulatory Exposure**:
Exempt — Crusoe is energy/cloud infrastructure, not an AI model provider. Not directly subject to EU AI Act GPAI obligations. Subject to US energy and environmental regulations for data center operations and natural gas usage. Increasing federal and state scrutiny of energy-intensive data centers may affect expansion plans.


### 2. Commonwealth Fusion Systems

*Source: Commonwealth_Fusion_Systems.json*


#### Identity

**Company Name**: Commonwealth Fusion Systems

**Headquarters**: Devens, Massachusetts, US

**Headquarters Region**: US

**Founded Year**: 2018


#### Value Chain

**Role In Value Chain**: energy_infrastructure

**Value Chain Position**:
CFS operates at the deepest upstream layer of the AI value chain — energy generation. It depends on advanced superconducting magnet technology (HTS magnets) and physics R&D from MIT. If successful, it will serve hyperscalers (Google, Microsoft) and data center operators who need massive, clean baseload power for AI workloads. Google has already signed a 200 MW Power Purchase Agreement for CFS's first ARC plant.

**Vertical Integration Level**: Single-layer


#### Products

**Key Products**:
- SPARC — compact tokamak fusion demonstration machine under construction in Devens, MA; targeting first plasma in 2026-2027 and net energy (Q>1) demonstration
- ARC — planned first commercial fusion power plant (400 MW capacity) in Chesterfield County, Virginia; targeting early 2030s grid connection
- High-Temperature Superconducting (HTS) Magnets — record-breaking 20-tesla large-bore magnets, the enabling technology for compact fusion


#### Business

**Revenue Or Valuation**:
Estimated valuation: $5-6B (based on ~$3B total funding and comparable analysis). Pre-commercial: negligible product revenue. Some estimates cite ~$5.9M peak revenue in 2023, likely from contracts, grants, or magnet technology licensing. Source: Growjo, CBInsights.

**Annual Recurring Revenue Arr**: Not applicable — CFS is a pre-commercial deep tech company. No recurring product revenue yet. Revenue will come from Power Purchase Agreements (PPAs) once ARC plant is operational in early 2030s.

**Funding Total And Last Round**:
Total raised: ~$3B (approximately one-third of all private fusion investment globally). Last round: $863M Series B2 (August 2025), following $1.8B Series B (2021). Investors include Google, Counterpoint Global (Morgan Stanley), NVentures (NVIDIA), Stanley Druckenmiller, Tiger Global, Breakthrough Energy Ventures, Temasek, Khosla Ventures.


#### Openness

**Open Vs Closed Spectrum**:
Closed — CFS is a proprietary hardware and energy company. Core magnet technology and fusion reactor designs are proprietary IP. Some academic publications with MIT, but commercial technology is fully closed.


#### Startup Relevance

**Relevance For Startups**:
CFS represents the long-term answer to the AI industry's biggest bottleneck: energy. For AI entrepreneurs, CFS matters as a signal of where the energy supply for AI infrastructure is heading. If fusion succeeds, it could dramatically reduce compute costs by providing abundant, clean baseload power — potentially reshaping the economics of AI training and inference. In the near term (2025-2030), CFS is not directly usable by startups, but its trajectory affects strategic planning: startups building for the 2030+ horizon should factor in the possibility of dramatically cheaper energy. Google's 200 MW PPA validates the commercial model. CFS also demonstrates the deep tech startup playbook: MIT spinout, massive R&D investment, long timelines, strategic corporate partnerships.

**Accessibility**:
Not accessible to startups as a product or service today. CFS is pre-commercial. First power delivery expected in early 2030s via ARC plant. No API, no cloud service, no platform to build on. Relevant only as a strategic signal for long-term planning and as a case study in deep tech entrepreneurship.

**Primary Model Dependency**:
None — CFS is a physical energy infrastructure company. Uses AI tools (Google DeepMind TORAX for plasma simulation, NVIDIA Omniverse/Siemens for digital twin) but does not depend on any foundation model for its core product.


#### Ecosystem

**Key Partnerships**:
- Google — 200 MW PPA for ARC plant, Google DeepMind collaboration on TORAX plasma simulation software, strategic investor
- MIT — original spinout, ongoing research collaboration on plasma physics and magnet technology
- NVIDIA (NVentures) — investor, Omniverse digital twin collaboration for SPARC
- Siemens — industrial software partnership for SPARC digital twin (announced CES 2026)
- Breakthrough Energy Ventures (Bill Gates) — early and continued investor

**Competitive Moat**:
CFS's moat rests on its proprietary high-temperature superconducting (HTS) magnet technology, which demonstrated a record 20-tesla field in 2021 — the key breakthrough enabling compact, commercially viable fusion. This gives CFS a multi-year lead over competitors. Deep MIT collaboration provides ongoing physics and engineering talent pipeline. Having raised ~$3B (one-third of all private fusion capital), CFS has a massive capital moat. The Google PPA and hyperscaler relationships provide demand-side validation. SPARC's construction progress (first magnet placed January 2026) is the most advanced demonstration of commercial fusion in the world.

**Data Moat Type**: Proprietary training data


#### Regulatory

**Regulatory Exposure**:
Exempt — CFS is an energy company, not an AI provider. Not subject to EU AI Act. Subject to nuclear regulatory frameworks (NRC in the US) for fusion reactor licensing, though fusion benefits from lighter regulation than fission as it produces no long-lived radioactive waste. CFS actively lobbies for favorable fusion regulatory treatment. Virginia site selection for ARC plant reflects supportive state regulatory environment.

## Layer 1 — Semiconductor / Hardware


### 3. ASML

*Source: ASML.json*


#### Identity

**Company Name**: ASML Holding N.V.

**Headquarters**: Veldhoven, Netherlands

**Headquarters Region**: EU

**Founded Year**: 1984


#### Value Chain

**Role In Value Chain**: hardware

**Value Chain Position**:
ASML is the deepest upstream player in the AI chip supply chain. It supplies EUV and DUV lithography machines to semiconductor foundries (TSMC, Samsung, Intel), which in turn fabricate chips for AI hardware designers (NVIDIA, AMD, Google). Without ASML's machines, no advanced AI chip (sub-7nm) can be physically manufactured. ASML depends on Zeiss (optics), Trumpf (lasers), and Cymer (light sources).

**Vertical Integration Level**: Single-layer


#### Products

**Key Products**:
- TWINSCAN NXE (EUV lithography systems, 0.33 NA) — workhorse for 7nm to 2nm chip manufacturing, ~€200M per unit
- TWINSCAN EXE (High-NA EUV lithography, 0.55 NA) — next-gen platform for sub-2nm nodes, ~€350M per unit, began high-volume shipments in early 2026
- Installed Base Management (IBM) — multi-decade service, upgrade, and maintenance contracts for the 5,500+ systems deployed globally, generating €8.2B in 2025


#### Business

**Revenue Or Valuation**: FY2025 revenue: €32.7 billion (net income €9.6 billion). Market cap ~$525 billion (Feb 2026). Source: ASML Q4 2025 press release.

**Funding Total And Last Round**: N/A — publicly traded since 1995 (Euronext Amsterdam: ASML, NASDAQ: ASML). Founded as Philips/ASM International joint venture in 1984, IPO in 1995.

**Business Model**:
Capital equipment sales (EUV/DUV systems at €150-350M each) plus recurring Installed Base Management services (maintenance, upgrades, spare parts). System sales ~75% of revenue, IBM services ~25%. Machines designed for 30+ year lifespans creating multi-decade service relationships. Also earns from metrology and inspection systems.

**Burn Rate And Runway**: N/A — profitable public company. 2025 net income €9.6B, gross margin 52.8%. R&D spend ~€4.5B (15% of revenue).


#### Openness

**Open Vs Closed Spectrum**: Closed — proprietary hardware and software. 2,000+ EUV patents. Technology is deeply protected trade secret and IP.


#### Startup Relevance

**Relevance For Startups**:
ASML is critical for entrepreneurs to understand as the ultimate bottleneck in the AI hardware supply chain. Every advanced AI chip — from NVIDIA's H100/B200 to Google's TPU — is manufactured using ASML's EUV machines. This creates three strategic implications: (1) Chip supply constraints: ASML's production capacity directly limits how many AI chips exist in the world, affecting GPU availability and pricing for AI startups; (2) Geopolitical risk: ASML is subject to US and Dutch export controls that restrict sales to China, reshaping the global AI competitive landscape; (3) Concentration risk: a single point of failure for the entire AI industry — any disruption to ASML (natural disaster, geopolitical event) would halt AI chip production globally. For entrepreneurs, ASML illustrates that the AI boom depends on physical infrastructure with very long lead times (EUV machines take 2+ years to build). ASML also actively supports startups through the Eindhoven Startup Alliance and HighTechXL accelerator, and made a notable $1.5B investment in Mistral AI in 2025.

**Accessibility**:
Not directly accessible to startups. ASML sells exclusively to large semiconductor foundries and IDMs (TSMC, Samsung, Intel, SK Hynix, Micron). Minimum machine cost is ~€150M. Startups interact with ASML's impact indirectly through chip availability and pricing. ASML does offer startup ecosystem support through the Eindhoven Startup Alliance for hardware/deep-tech startups in the Netherlands.

**Primary Model Dependency**: None — ASML is a hardware equipment manufacturer, not dependent on any AI foundation models.


#### Ecosystem

**Key Partnerships**:
- Zeiss (Carl Zeiss SMT) — exclusive supplier of EUV optics, 15+ year co-development relationship. ASML owns ~25% of Zeiss SMT.
- Trumpf — exclusive supplier of EUV laser/light source systems (CO2 laser drive technology)
- TSMC — largest customer, co-development of lithography roadmap
- Samsung Foundry — major EUV customer
- Intel — major customer and first recipient of High-NA EUV (EXE:5200B)
- imec (Belgium) — joint High-NA EUV research lab for ecosystem R&D

**Competitive Moat**:
ASML has arguably the strongest competitive moat in the entire technology sector. Key elements: (1) 30+ years and tens of billions of cumulative R&D creating irreproducible institutional knowledge; (2) 2,000+ EUV patents; (3) Exclusive supplier relationships (Zeiss optics, Trumpf lasers) — these suppliers only make components for ASML; (4) Each EUV machine has 100,000+ components from 800+ suppliers globally; (5) 100% market share in EUV systems — literally no competitor can produce a working EUV lithography machine; (6) Massive installed base creating 30-year service revenue streams; (7) Network effects: every chip designer optimizes for ASML's lithography capabilities. Even with unlimited funding, replicating ASML would take 15-20 years minimum.

**Data Moat Type**: None — ASML's moat is based on proprietary hardware IP, manufacturing know-how, and exclusive supply chain relationships rather than data.


#### Regulatory

**Regulatory Exposure**:
Exempt from EU AI Act (hardware equipment manufacturer, not an AI system provider). However, heavily exposed to semiconductor export controls: Dutch government enacted the Regulation on Advanced Production Equipment for Semiconductors (June 2023) requiring export licenses for advanced lithography equipment. US Foreign Direct Product Rule (FDPR) also applies since ~10% of ASML's technology is American-origin. China represented 29% of ASML's 2023 revenue — export restrictions have material business impact. ASML stated the impact of updated 2024 restrictions falls within its 2025 outlook.


### 4. TSMC

*Source: TSMC.json*


#### Identity

**Company Name**: Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company (TSMC)

**Headquarters**: Hsinchu, Taiwan

**Headquarters Region**: Other

**Founded Year**: 1987


#### Value Chain

**Role In Value Chain**: hardware

**Value Chain Position**:
TSMC is the critical manufacturing link between chip designers and end users. It depends upstream on equipment suppliers (ASML for lithography, Applied Materials, Lam Research for etching/deposition, ASML for metrology) and serves downstream chip designers who lack their own fabs: NVIDIA, AMD, Apple, Broadcom, Qualcomm, Google, Amazon, and Microsoft. TSMC fabricates ~90% of the world's most advanced AI chips (sub-7nm), making it the single most important manufacturing node in the entire AI value chain.

**Vertical Integration Level**: Single-layer


#### Products

**Key Products**:
- Advanced node foundry services (3nm N3/N3P/N3E, 5nm N5/N4P) — fabricates cutting-edge AI accelerators (NVIDIA Blackwell, AMD MI300, Apple M-series). 3nm and 5nm together account for ~60% of total revenue.
- 2nm (N2) process — began mass production Q4 2025 with backside power delivery, targeting next-gen AI and mobile chips
- Advanced packaging (CoWoS, InFO, SoIC) — critical for AI chip integration, packaging NVIDIA's multi-die GPU architectures. Demand-constrained; TSMC rapidly expanding CoWoS capacity.


#### Business

**Revenue Or Valuation**: FY2025 revenue: NT$3.81 trillion (~US$122.9 billion), up 31.6% YoY. Market cap ~$1.81 trillion (Feb 2026). Source: TSMC investor relations, companiesmarketcap.com.

**Funding Total And Last Round**: N/A — publicly traded since 1994 (TWSE: 2330, NYSE: TSM). Originally funded as joint venture between Taiwan government (ITRI), Philips, and private investors. No venture funding history.

**Business Model**:
Pure-play semiconductor foundry: charges per-wafer fabrication fees that vary by process node (advanced nodes command significant premiums — TSMC implementing 16-24% price increases for 3nm N3P process). Revenue breakdown by platform: High Performance Computing/AI 57%, Smartphone 30%, IoT 6%, Automotive 4%, DCE 3%. Also earns from mask-making, testing, and advanced packaging services. Strong pricing power due to monopoly position at advanced nodes.


#### Openness

**Open Vs Closed Spectrum**:
Closed — proprietary process technology and manufacturing know-how. TSMC's process design kits (PDKs) are provided to customers under strict NDA. Manufacturing recipes and yield optimization techniques are core trade secrets.


#### Startup Relevance

**Relevance For Startups**:
TSMC is essential for entrepreneurs to understand for several reasons: (1) Every AI chip your startup uses was likely made by TSMC — NVIDIA GPUs, AMD GPUs, Apple Neural Engine, Google TPUs, and AWS Graviton chips are all fabricated at TSMC. Understanding TSMC's capacity constraints explains why GPU shortages happen; (2) The fabless model that TSMC pioneered is why AI chip startups can exist — companies like Cerebras, Groq, and SambaNova design custom AI chips and outsource manufacturing to TSMC, avoiding the $20B+ cost of building a fab; (3) TSMC's pricing and capacity allocation decisions directly impact the cost and availability of AI compute for startups; (4) Geopolitical risk — TSMC's concentration in Taiwan (within missile range of China) represents a systemic risk for the entire AI industry, driving the US CHIPS Act and geographic diversification (Arizona fabs, ESMC in Dresden). For AI hardware startups specifically, getting TSMC fab allocation is a critical milestone and competitive differentiator.

**Accessibility**:
Challenging but possible for funded startups. TSMC serves fabless chip design companies of various sizes through tiered programs. Startups can access TSMC manufacturing through: (1) Multi-project wafer (MPW) / shuttle runs that share wafer costs across multiple designs — significantly reducing entry cost for prototyping; (2) TSMC's Open Innovation Platform (OIP) ecosystem with design tools and IP libraries; (3) Older nodes (28nm, 16nm) are more accessible and affordable than cutting-edge 3nm/2nm. However, advanced node allocation is severely capacity-constrained and prioritized for large customers. Typical NRE (non-recurring engineering) costs for a new chip design at 3nm can exceed $500M. Startups typically need significant venture funding ($50M+) to engage TSMC for custom chip production.

**Primary Model Dependency**: None — TSMC is a semiconductor manufacturer, not dependent on any AI foundation models.


#### Ecosystem

**Key Partnerships**:
- Apple — largest customer (~25% of revenue in 2023), exclusive manufacturer of all Apple silicon (A-series, M-series chips)
- NVIDIA — fabricates all NVIDIA AI GPUs (H100, B200, GB200), partnership since 1995. NVIDIA orders ~11% of TSMC revenue.
- AMD — fabricates all AMD CPUs and GPUs (Zen 5, MI300X AI accelerators)
- ASML — critical equipment supplier; TSMC is ASML's largest EUV customer and co-develops lithography roadmap
- Broadcom, Qualcomm, MediaTek — major mobile and networking chip customers
- ESMC joint venture (Dresden, Germany) with Bosch, Infineon, NXP — TSMC holds 70% stake, EU's first FinFET-capable foundry, production targeted for 2027
- US CHIPS Act — building three fabs in Arizona plus R&D center and advanced packaging facilities ($100B+ total investment)

**Competitive Moat**:
TSMC's moat is multi-layered: (1) Process technology leadership — consistently first to mass-produce at new nodes (3nm, now 2nm), with 1-2 year lead over Samsung and Intel; (2) Manufacturing yield excellence — consistently achieves higher yields than competitors, meaning more working chips per wafer; (3) Customer trust and ecosystem — decades of never competing with its own customers (unlike Samsung which makes its own chips), earning the 'pure-play' trust premium; (4) Scale — 72% foundry market share creates volume-driven cost advantages and R&D reinvestment capacity; (5) Talent concentration — deep pool of semiconductor engineers in Taiwan's Hsinchu Science Park; (6) Advanced packaging leadership (CoWoS) — critical for multi-die AI chips, currently supply-constrained; (7) $38-56B annual capex creates massive barriers to entry. Intel and Samsung have spent billions trying to compete and remain far behind.

**Data Moat Type**: None — TSMC's moat derives from proprietary process technology, manufacturing know-how, scale economies, and customer trust rather than data.


#### Regulatory

**Regulatory Exposure**:
Exempt from EU AI Act (semiconductor manufacturer, not an AI system provider). However, heavily exposed to geopolitical and export control regulations: (1) US semiconductor export controls restrict TSMC from manufacturing advanced chips for certain Chinese entities (Huawei, etc.); (2) US CHIPS Act provides ~$6.6B in subsidies for Arizona fabs but comes with restrictions on expanding advanced manufacturing in China for 10 years; (3) EU Chips Act provides €5B+ in subsidies for the Dresden ESMC fab; (4) Taiwan Strait geopolitical risk is the single largest regulatory/political risk factor — potential Chinese military action could disrupt global AI chip supply. TSMC has been called 'the silicon shield' protecting Taiwan's strategic importance.


### 5. NVIDIA

*Source: NVIDIA.json*


#### Identity

**Company Name**: NVIDIA Corporation

**Headquarters**: Santa Clara, USA

**Headquarters Region**: US

**Founded Year**: 1993


#### Value Chain

**Role In Value Chain**: hardware

**Value Chain Position**:
Foundational hardware layer. NVIDIA depends on TSMC for chip fabrication and supplies GPUs to cloud providers (AWS, Azure, GCP, OCI), AI labs (OpenAI, Meta, Google DeepMind), enterprises, and sovereign AI projects. Its CUDA software platform and networking (InfiniBand/NVLink) create deep vertical integration from silicon to developer tooling.

**Vertical Integration Level**: Multi-layer


#### Products

**Key Products**:
- Data Center GPUs (H100, H200, B200, GB200 Blackwell, upcoming Rubin): dominant AI training and inference accelerators powering the majority of global AI workloads
- CUDA Platform and Software Stack (cuDNN, TensorRT, NIM microservices, Triton Inference Server): the de facto programming model and library ecosystem for GPU computing with 98% developer adoption
- DGX Systems and DGX Cloud: turnkey AI supercomputer systems and cloud-based GPU clusters starting at ~$37,000/month for enterprise AI development


#### Business

**Revenue Or Valuation**: FY2025 (ended Jan 2025) revenue: $130.5B (+114% YoY). FY2026 Q3 quarterly revenue: $57.0B. Market cap: ~$4.5 trillion (Feb 2026), making it the world's most valuable company.

**Funding Total And Last Round**: Public company (NASDAQ: NVDA) since 1999. No venture funding rounds applicable. Market cap ~$4.5T (Feb 2026).

**Business Model**:
Primarily hardware sales (GPUs, networking equipment, DGX systems). Data Center segment generates ~88% of revenue. Supplemented by software licensing (CUDA ecosystem, NVIDIA AI Enterprise), cloud services (DGX Cloud), IP licensing (~$152M), and automotive/professional visualization segments. Growing recurring revenue from NVIDIA AI Enterprise subscriptions and NIM microservices.


#### Openness

**Open Vs Closed Spectrum**:
Closed / Hybrid. Core CUDA toolkit is proprietary (free to use but locked to NVIDIA GPUs). Some components recently open-sourced (CUDA Tile IR under Apache 2.0, GPU kernel drivers). Key libraries (cuDNN, TensorRT) remain proprietary. Strategic open-source contributions to PyTorch and other frameworks.


#### Startup Relevance

**Relevance For Startups**:
NVIDIA is the unavoidable infrastructure layer for virtually all AI startups. Its GPUs are the default hardware for training and deploying AI models, and its CUDA ecosystem is the standard programming environment. Entrepreneurs should understand: (1) CUDA lock-in means switching to AMD or custom silicon carries significant migration cost, a factor in long-term infrastructure planning; (2) GPU supply constraints have been a major bottleneck for AI startups since 2023 -- access to H100/B200 allocation can be a competitive advantage; (3) NVIDIA's pricing power directly affects AI startup unit economics (compute is often the largest cost line); (4) The Blackwell generation and upcoming Rubin platform will shift cost/performance ratios, affecting build-vs-buy decisions; (5) NVIDIA's ecosystem (NIM, Triton, TensorRT) can accelerate time-to-market for inference optimization but deepen vendor dependency; (6) The $20B Groq asset acquisition signals NVIDIA is moving aggressively into specialized inference, potentially commoditizing standalone inference startups.

**Accessibility**:
High accessibility through cloud providers: NVIDIA GPUs are available on all major clouds (AWS, Azure, GCP, OCI) at $2-4/hour for H100s via spot/on-demand instances. Free CUDA toolkit download. NVIDIA Inception program offers startups up to $500K in cloud credits, technical support, and co-marketing. DGX Cloud from $37K/month for dedicated clusters. Developer tools (CUDA, cuDNN, TensorRT) are free to download. However, direct GPU purchase remains expensive (H100 ~$25-40K each).

**Primary Model Dependency**: None. NVIDIA is a hardware/platform provider, not dependent on any foundation model.


#### Ecosystem

**Key Partnerships**:
All major hyperscalers (AWS, Microsoft Azure, Google Cloud, Oracle Cloud) deploy NVIDIA GPUs and Blackwell/Rubin platforms. Strategic investments in CoreWeave (GPU cloud provider), OpenAI ($100B infrastructure partnership for 10GW data centers), and AI startups via the Inception program. Groq asset acquisition ($20B, Dec 2025) for inference technology. Partnerships with Dell, Lenovo, HPE for server systems. TSMC for chip fabrication.

**Competitive Moat**:
CUDA ecosystem lock-in: 18+ years of software libraries, developer tools, and millions of trained developers create massive switching costs. Hardware performance leadership: consistent generational leaps (Hopper -> Blackwell -> Rubin) maintain 1-2 year lead. Full-stack integration: silicon + networking (NVLink, InfiniBand) + software (CUDA, TensorRT, NIM) + cloud (DGX Cloud) creates a flywheel where hardware sales fund software R&D, which drives more hardware adoption. Over $500B in confirmed orders for Blackwell and Rubin GPUs.

**Data Moat Type**: CUDA lock-in


#### Regulatory

**Regulatory Exposure**:
Exempt (hardware provider). As a semiconductor company, NVIDIA is not directly regulated as a GPAI provider or high-risk AI system operator under the EU AI Act. However, NVIDIA benefits from EU sovereign AI requirements by supplying hardware for EU-compliant AI infrastructure. US export controls on advanced chips to China affect NVIDIA's revenue (China-specific products like H800/A800 created to comply). NVIDIA is actively investing in European AI infrastructure to position as backbone for regulated EU AI market.

#### Identity

**Company Name**: Advanced Micro Devices, Inc. (AMD)

#### Value Chain

**Role In Value Chain**: hardware

**Value Chain Position**:
Hardware challenger in AI accelerators and CPUs. AMD depends on TSMC for chip fabrication (same as NVIDIA). Supplies GPUs (Instinct MI series) and CPUs (EPYC) to cloud providers (Oracle, Microsoft Azure, AWS), AI labs (Meta, OpenAI), and enterprises. Also provides FPGAs and adaptive SoCs (from Xilinx acquisition) for edge and embedded AI. Positioned as the primary alternative to NVIDIA in the data center GPU market.

**Vertical Integration Level**: Multi-layer


#### Products

**Key Products**:
- Instinct MI300X/MI350/MI450 AI Accelerators: data center GPUs for AI training and inference, with MI350 delivering 35x inference performance over MI300X and 288GB HBM3E memory. MI450 with rack-scale Helios architecture expected 2026.
- EPYC Server CPUs (Turin/5th Gen): dominant in server market share vs Intel, powering data center compute alongside GPUs. Record $16.6B data center segment revenue in 2025.
- ROCm Open Software Platform: open-source GPU computing stack (v7 released Sept 2025) supporting PyTorch, TensorFlow, JAX, and vLLM. AMD's answer to CUDA, with growing but still smaller ecosystem.


#### Business

**Revenue Or Valuation**: Full year 2025 revenue: $34.6B (+34% YoY). Data Center segment: $16.6B (record, +32% YoY). Market cap: ~$340B (Feb 2026).

**Funding Total And Last Round**: Public company (NASDAQ: AMD) since 1972. Acquired Xilinx for ~$50B (all-stock, completed Feb 2022). No venture funding rounds applicable. Market cap ~$340B (Feb 2026).

**Business Model**:
Hardware sales across three segments: Data Center ($16.6B in 2025, including Instinct GPUs, EPYC CPUs, FPGAs, DPUs), Client and Gaming ($14.6B, including Ryzen CPUs, Radeon GPUs, semi-custom console SoCs), and Embedded ($3.5B, including FPGAs and adaptive SoCs from Xilinx). Revenue is primarily from chip sales to OEMs, cloud providers, and enterprises. Growing AI accelerator revenue expected to reach tens of billions by 2027.


#### Openness

**Open Vs Closed Spectrum**:
Hybrid / Open-source leaning. ROCm is fully open-source (drivers, compilers, libraries, runtimes) and community-driven. AMD actively promotes an open AI ecosystem strategy as a competitive differentiator vs NVIDIA's proprietary CUDA. Hardware specifications and ISA documentation are more openly available than NVIDIA's. However, chip designs themselves are proprietary.


#### Startup Relevance

**Relevance For Startups**:
AMD is increasingly relevant as the credible alternative to NVIDIA for AI compute, offering entrepreneurs options to reduce vendor lock-in and potentially lower costs. Key considerations: (1) MI300X/MI350 GPUs are 15-40% cheaper than equivalent NVIDIA GPUs while narrowing the performance gap to 10-30%, improving AI startup unit economics; (2) ROCm's open-source nature means no licensing costs and growing framework support (PyTorch, vLLM day-zero support), reducing dependency on a single vendor; (3) Major cloud availability is expanding -- Oracle will deploy 50,000 MI450 GPUs, Microsoft Azure offers MI300X instances, and AMD Developer Cloud provides free access for prototyping; (4) OpenAI's $90B committed GPU purchase from AMD signals enterprise validation and will drive ROCm ecosystem maturity; (5) For startups concerned about NVIDIA supply constraints or pricing power, AMD provides negotiating leverage and a realistic Plan B; (6) The Xilinx FPGA portfolio enables edge AI deployment for IoT and embedded startups; (7) However, the ROCm ecosystem is still less mature -- startups should budget for additional engineering time when choosing AMD over NVIDIA.

**Accessibility**:
Growing accessibility. AMD Developer Cloud offers free access to MI300X GPUs with GitHub login, no credit card required. ROCm is free and open-source. Cloud availability through Microsoft Azure, Oracle Cloud, and AWS. AMD provided 100,000 hours of free cloud compute to Indian startups/researchers in 2025. MI300X available on specialized GPU cloud providers. However, fewer cloud instance options and smaller community than NVIDIA CUDA ecosystem. PyTorch and vLLM work out-of-the-box on ROCm 7.

**Primary Model Dependency**: None. AMD is a hardware/platform provider, not dependent on any foundation model.


#### Ecosystem

**Key Partnerships**:
OpenAI: strategic partnership for $90B in AMD Instinct GPU purchases (6 GW deployment starting H2 2026). Oracle Cloud: first hyperscaler to offer MI450 superclusters (50,000 GPUs, Q3 2026). Microsoft Azure: MI300X and MI350 deployment commitments. Meta: close collaboration on AI hardware roadmap (MI350, MI400 series). Supercomputing: AMD powers Frontier and El Capitan, two of the world's most powerful supercomputers. AI companies: Fireworks AI, Cohere use AMD GPUs in production.

**Competitive Moat**:
Open ecosystem strategy: ROCm's open-source nature attracts developers and organizations seeking to avoid CUDA lock-in. Price-performance advantage: 15-40% cost savings on comparable GPU performance. Broad compute portfolio: CPUs (EPYC) + GPUs (Instinct) + FPGAs (Xilinx) under one vendor simplifies procurement for data centers. Validated by hyperscalers: Oracle, Microsoft, Meta commitments de-risk adoption. However, moat is weaker than NVIDIA's -- AMD must continuously close the software ecosystem gap and maintain competitive hardware cadence.

**Data Moat Type**: None


#### Regulatory

**Regulatory Exposure**:
Exempt (hardware provider). As a semiconductor company, AMD is not directly regulated as a GPAI provider or high-risk AI system under the EU AI Act. Similar to NVIDIA, AMD benefits from sovereign AI infrastructure demand in Europe. Subject to US export controls on advanced chips to China. AMD has created region-specific product variants to comply with export restrictions.


### 7. Intel

*Source: Intel.json*


#### Identity

**Company Name**: Intel Corporation

#### Value Chain

**Role In Value Chain**: hardware

**Value Chain Position**:
Foundational hardware and chip manufacturing layer. Intel designs CPUs (Xeon for data centers, Core for clients) and AI accelerators (Gaudi 3, upcoming Jaguar Shores), and operates its own foundries (Intel Foundry Services) competing with TSMC. Intel depends on ASML for EUV lithography equipment and supplies chips to cloud providers (AWS, Microsoft Azure), OEMs (Dell, HP, Lenovo), and enterprises. Its foundry ambitions aim to manufacture chips for external customers including Microsoft, Amazon, and potentially other AI companies on Intel 18A and 14A process nodes.

**Vertical Integration Level**: Multi-layer


#### Products

**Key Products**:
- Xeon Processors (Xeon 6, upcoming Diamond Rapids): server CPUs powering the majority of enterprise data centers and cloud infrastructure, with integrated AI acceleration features
- Gaudi 3 AI Accelerators: data center AI accelerators positioned as a cost-effective, open-ecosystem alternative to NVIDIA GPUs, offering ~70% better price-performance on LLM inference vs H100. Gaudi line being retired; succeeded by Jaguar Shores (rack-scale AI system, 2026) and Crescent Island (inference GPU, 2026)
- Intel Foundry Services (IFS): external chip manufacturing on Intel 3, Intel 18A (1.8nm), and upcoming Intel 14A process nodes, with customers including Microsoft (Maia 2 AI chip) and AWS (custom Xeon 6 and AI fabric chip)


#### Business

**Revenue Or Valuation**:
FY2025 annual revenue: $52.9B (-0.5% YoY). Q4 2025 revenue: $13.7B. GAAP EPS: -$0.06 for the year. Market cap: ~$240-250B (Feb 2026). Significant restructuring year with Altera divestiture and U.S. government equity stake.

**Funding Total And Last Round**:
Public company (NASDAQ: INTC) since 1971. U.S. government acquired 9.9% equity stake for $8.9B (Aug 2025) through CHIPS Act, purchasing 433.3M shares at $20.47/share. Total government investment: $11.1B including prior CHIPS grants of $2.2B. Additional EU Chips Act subsidies committed for European fab expansion.

**Business Model**:
Semiconductor product sales (Xeon CPUs, client processors, AI accelerators) across Client Computing Group and Data Center & AI Group. Foundry services (manufacturing chips for external customers). Software licensing (oneAPI ecosystem). Revenue split roughly: client computing ~50%, data center & AI ~30%, foundry and other ~20%. Altera (FPGA) business deconsolidated in Sept 2025 after selling 51% stake.


#### Openness

**Open Vs Closed Spectrum**:
Hybrid. Gaudi software stack is open-source, supporting PyTorch natively and integrating with Hugging Face. oneAPI is an open, cross-architecture programming model (contrasting with NVIDIA's proprietary CUDA). Intel contributes to open-source AI frameworks and promotes open Ethernet networking standards. However, chip designs and foundry process technology remain proprietary. More open than NVIDIA on the software side, but less dominant.


#### Startup Relevance

**Relevance For Startups**:
Intel matters to AI entrepreneurs for several reasons: (1) Gaudi 3 offers significantly better price-performance than NVIDIA H100 for LLM inference workloads, which can materially reduce compute costs for inference-heavy startups — though the Gaudi line is being retired, making long-term commitment risky; (2) Intel's open-source software stack and oneAPI reduce vendor lock-in compared to CUDA, giving startups more flexibility to switch hardware; (3) Intel Foundry Services could become a critical second source for custom chip manufacturing, relevant for startups building custom silicon or for geopolitical supply chain diversification; (4) Intel's Xeon processors remain the default enterprise server CPU, meaning most startup cloud workloads already run on Intel hardware; (5) The U.S. government's strategic investment signals Intel will be sustained as a 'national champion' — important for startups in regulated or defense-adjacent markets; (6) CEO Lip-Bu Tan has acknowledged Intel is 'too late' for AI training, focusing instead on inference and foundry — startups should factor in Intel's shifting AI strategy when making hardware bets; (7) The upcoming Jaguar Shores and Crescent Island products (2026) represent Intel's next attempt at AI hardware relevance, but remain unproven.

**Accessibility**:
High accessibility for Xeon processors via all major cloud providers. Gaudi 3 available on IBM Cloud (50% off first 6 months), Dell PowerEdge, HPE, and Supermicro systems. Open-source Gaudi software stack with Hugging Face integration lowers migration barrier. Intel Liftoff program provides startup credits and technical support. However, Gaudi ecosystem is much smaller than CUDA, with fewer pre-optimized models and community resources. Direct Gaudi 3 hardware purchase pricing is competitive vs NVIDIA but availability is more limited.

**Primary Model Dependency**: None. Intel is a hardware/platform provider, not dependent on any foundation model.


#### Ecosystem

**Key Partnerships**:
Microsoft (custom Maia 2 AI accelerator on Intel 18A foundry process), Amazon AWS (custom Xeon 6 and AI fabric chip manufacturing), Dell Technologies and HPE (Gaudi 3 server systems), IBM Cloud (Gaudi 3 cloud hosting), U.S. Government (9.9% equity stake, CHIPS Act national champion). Foundry packaging customers include NVIDIA, Qualcomm, and Tesla. European fab partnerships with Germany (~$11B subsidies for Magdeburg facility) and Ireland (Fab34 EUV manufacturing). SK hynix for HBM4 memory (Jaguar Shores).

**Competitive Moat**:
Vertical integration of chip design + manufacturing (only major company doing both at leading edge). x86 instruction set dominance in enterprise computing creates massive installed base and switching costs. Intel Foundry is the only credible Western alternative to TSMC for leading-edge chip manufacturing, making it strategically irreplaceable for U.S./EU semiconductor sovereignty. However, the moat has weakened significantly: TSMC leads in manufacturing, NVIDIA dominates AI accelerators, and AMD has eroded Xeon market share. Intel's moat is increasingly geopolitical/strategic rather than purely technological.

**Data Moat Type**: Distribution


#### Regulatory

**Regulatory Exposure**:
Exempt (hardware provider). As a semiconductor manufacturer, Intel is not directly regulated under the EU AI Act as a GPAI provider or high-risk AI system operator. Intel is a primary beneficiary of both the U.S. CHIPS Act ($11.1B total investment) and EU Chips Act (subsidies for European fab expansion). Subject to U.S. export controls on advanced chips to China. Intel's European manufacturing presence (Ireland Fab34, planned Magdeburg facility) positions it favorably for EU semiconductor sovereignty requirements.

*Source: Broadcom.json*


#### Identity

**Company Name**: Broadcom Inc.

#### Value Chain

**Role In Value Chain**: hardware

**Value Chain Position**:
Critical AI hardware enabler sitting between chip fabrication (depends on TSMC for manufacturing) and hyperscale cloud providers (serves Google, Meta, OpenAI, Anthropic). Broadcom designs custom AI accelerators (XPUs/ASICs) to hyperscaler specifications and provides the networking silicon (Tomahawk, Jericho switches) that interconnects GPU/AI clusters. VMware software stack adds a cloud infrastructure layer. Broadcom does not manufacture chips itself — it is a fabless semiconductor company relying on TSMC and other foundries.

**Vertical Integration Level**: Multi-layer


#### Products

**Key Products**:
- Custom AI Accelerators (XPUs): bespoke ASIC chips co-designed with hyperscalers — Google TPU v7 (Ironwood), Meta MTIA v4 (Santa Barbara), OpenAI Titan, Anthropic custom chips. Broadcom holds ~70% of the custom AI ASIC market. AI semiconductor revenue: $20B in FY2025, guided to double in FY2026
- AI Networking Silicon (Tomahawk 5/6, Jericho3-AI): Ethernet switch chips for AI cluster interconnect. Tomahawk 6 delivers 102.4 Tbps bandwidth, becoming the gold standard for high-density AI networking. AI switch backlog exceeds $10B
- VMware Cloud Foundation (VCF): enterprise cloud infrastructure and virtualization platform acquired for $69B in 2023. Now generating ~$27B annual revenue in infrastructure software with high recurring subscription model


#### Business

**Revenue Or Valuation**:
FY2025 (ended Oct 2025) consolidated revenue: $64B (+24% YoY), a record. AI semiconductor revenue: $20B (+65% YoY). Q4 FY2025 revenue: $18B (+28% YoY). Adjusted EBITDA: $43B. Market cap: ~$1.58T (Feb 2026).

**Funding Total And Last Round**: Public company (NASDAQ: AVGO). No venture funding applicable. Financed $69B VMware acquisition (closed Nov 2023) through combination of cash, stock, and $32B in debt. Market cap ~$1.58T (Feb 2026).

**Business Model**:
Two-segment model: (1) Semiconductor Solutions (~$37B, 58% of revenue): custom AI ASICs designed to hyperscaler specs, networking switch silicon, broadband/wireless chips. Revenue is transactional hardware sales but with long-term design-win contracts and multi-year backlogs. (2) Infrastructure Software (~$27B, 42% of revenue): VMware virtualization/cloud platform converted from perpetual licenses to bundled subscriptions requiring multi-year commitments. Extremely high-margin business (adjusted EBITDA margin ~67%). Free cash flow: $26.9B in FY2025.

**Burn Rate And Runway**: Not applicable. Highly profitable public company. FY2025 adjusted EBITDA: $43B. Free cash flow: $26.9B. Aggressively paying down VMware acquisition debt.


#### Openness

**Open Vs Closed Spectrum**:
Hybrid. Broadcom champions open Ethernet standards for AI networking (Ultra Ethernet Consortium) as an alternative to NVIDIA's proprietary InfiniBand — this openness is strategically motivated. VMware Cloud Foundation supports SONiC (open-source network OS). However, custom ASIC designs are deeply proprietary and locked to specific hyperscaler customers. VMware licensing has become more restrictive post-acquisition (bundled subscriptions, elimination of perpetual licenses). Overall: open on networking standards, closed on chip IP and increasingly closed on software licensing.


#### Startup Relevance

**Relevance For Startups**:
Broadcom is mostly invisible to startups but deeply shapes their infrastructure costs and options: (1) The custom ASIC trend Broadcom leads means hyperscalers are building alternatives to NVIDIA GPUs — this could eventually reduce GPU pricing and improve cloud compute economics for startups; (2) Broadcom's Ethernet networking silicon powers the AI cluster interconnects that startups rent on cloud platforms — the shift from InfiniBand to Ethernet could reduce cloud provider costs, potentially flowing to lower pricing; (3) VMware licensing changes post-acquisition have dramatically increased infrastructure costs (800-1,500% increases reported) for enterprises using VMware — startups on VMware should evaluate migration to alternatives (Proxmox, OpenStack, Kubernetes); (4) The OpenAI-Broadcom partnership (10GW of custom Titan chips, deployments starting H2 2026) signals that the largest AI companies are moving toward custom silicon, which may commoditize general-purpose GPU demand over time; (5) Broadcom's dominance in networking silicon means any startup building AI infrastructure will indirectly depend on Broadcom hardware; (6) For hardware startups: Broadcom's 70% share of custom ASIC market makes it a potential design partner, but its focus is exclusively on hyperscaler-scale customers, not startups.

**Accessibility**:
Very low direct accessibility for startups. Broadcom's custom ASIC business serves only hyperscale customers (Google, Meta, OpenAI, Anthropic) with billion-dollar-scale engagements. Networking silicon is sold through OEMs and cloud providers, not directly to startups. VMware products are accessible but expensive — pricing increases post-acquisition have pushed many smaller companies away. Startups interact with Broadcom indirectly through cloud infrastructure pricing rather than directly. No meaningful startup program.

**Primary Model Dependency**: None. Broadcom is a hardware/infrastructure provider, not dependent on any foundation model. Its custom ASIC clients (Google, OpenAI, Anthropic, Meta) build their own models.


#### Ecosystem

**Key Partnerships**:
Google (co-designed TPU v7 Ironwood, decade-long partnership), Meta (MTIA v4 custom AI accelerator), OpenAI (10GW custom Titan chip deployment, $100-200B multi-year deal starting H2 2026), Anthropic ($10B custom chip deal for 400K TPU v7 Ironwoods), TSMC (primary manufacturing partner for all Broadcom chips). Ultra Ethernet Consortium membership for open AI networking standards. Dell, HPE for enterprise networking equipment. Apple (long-standing wireless chip partnership).

**Competitive Moat**:
Custom ASIC co-design lock-in: once a hyperscaler designs its AI architecture around Broadcom's custom silicon, switching costs are enormous (years of co-development, tight hardware-software integration). Google TPU partnership spans nearly a decade. Networking silicon dominance: Tomahawk series is the de facto standard for Ethernet AI cluster switching. VMware installed base: millions of enterprise customers locked into VMware virtualization, making migration extremely costly despite price increases. Hock Tan's acquisition playbook: acquire critical infrastructure companies, cut costs, raise prices on locked-in customers. 70% share of custom AI ASIC market with highest-value customers.

**Data Moat Type**: Distribution


#### Regulatory

**Regulatory Exposure**:
Exempt from EU AI Act (hardware/infrastructure provider, not a GPAI provider or high-risk AI system operator). However, Broadcom faces significant EU antitrust/competition scrutiny over VMware licensing practices — CISPE (European cloud provider trade group) has filed legal action seeking annulment of the EU Commission's approval of the VMware acquisition, citing 800-1,500% price increases and anticompetitive bundling. Bloomberg reports (Feb 2026) mounting EU scrutiny over VMware licensing curbs. This regulatory risk is competition law, not AI Act.

Alternative AI hardware layer challenging NVIDIA's GPU dominance. **Business Model**:
Hardware system sales (CS-3 systems), cloud-based inference service (pay-per-token API), CSoft software platform licensing, and professional services (data preparation, model design, training support, optimization). The $10B+ OpenAI deal (750MW of compute through 2028) represents a shift toward large-scale infrastructure-as-a-service contracts.


#### Openness

**Open Vs Closed Spectrum**:
Hybrid. Cerebras Inference Cloud runs open-weight models (Llama, DeepSeek, Qwen, Mistral) and actively supports open-source ecosystem. Founding member of AI Alliance (with IBM and Meta). However, the WSE hardware architecture and CSoft software platform are proprietary.


#### Startup Relevance

**Relevance For Startups**:
Cerebras matters for AI entrepreneurs in three ways: (1) Inference cost and speed -- Cerebras Inference delivers up to 21x faster inference than GPU clusters at competitive pricing ($0.10-$0.60 per million tokens), which can dramatically improve user experience for real-time AI applications and reduce latency-sensitive workload costs; (2) Alternative to NVIDIA lock-in -- as the most credible non-GPU AI hardware company, Cerebras offers a hedge against NVIDIA supply constraints and CUDA dependency, relevant for startups planning long-term infrastructure strategy; (3) The OpenAI partnership validates wafer-scale technology for production workloads at massive scale, signaling that the AI hardware market is diversifying beyond GPUs. Startups building inference-heavy applications (chatbots, coding assistants, real-time translation) should evaluate Cerebras Inference as a faster alternative. The key risk: Cerebras remains pre-IPO with heavy customer concentration (G42 was >50% of revenue historically, now shifting to OpenAI), and its long-term viability depends on executing the $10B OpenAI contract.

**Accessibility**:
High accessibility for inference workloads. Free tier available at cloud.cerebras.ai. Developer tier starts at $0.10/M tokens (Llama 8B) and $0.60/M tokens (Llama 70B). OpenAI-compatible API makes migration trivial. Available on AWS Marketplace. Code Pro ($50/month) and Code Max ($200/month) plans for developers. Enterprise tier with custom SLAs available on request. Hardware purchase is enterprise-only and requires direct engagement.

**Primary Model Dependency**: None. Cerebras is a hardware/infrastructure provider. Runs open-weight models (Llama, DeepSeek, Qwen, Mistral) on its platform but does not depend on any single foundation model.


#### Ecosystem

**Key Partnerships**:
OpenAI: $10B+ multi-year deal to deploy 750MW of wafer-scale inference systems (2026-2028), the largest high-speed AI inference deployment in the world. G42/Core42 (Abu Dhabi): built Condor Galaxy supercomputers (8 exaFLOPS), though G42 recently exited as an investor. AI Alliance founding member alongside IBM and Meta. TSMC: sole fabrication partner for WSE chips. AMD: strategic investor in Series H round. Mayo Clinic, GlaxoSmithKline, and Meta among customers.

**Competitive Moat**:
Wafer-scale integration technology: the WSE-3 is 57x larger than the largest GPU, eliminating multi-chip networking overhead and providing 7,000x more memory bandwidth than HBM3e GPU systems. This architectural advantage enables single-device simplicity for models up to 24T parameters with no distributed systems code required, versus tens of thousands of lines of code for equivalent GPU clusters. Over a decade of manufacturing know-how for defect-tolerant wafer-scale chips creates a significant engineering barrier to entry. The OpenAI contract provides a demand moat securing revenue through 2028.

**Data Moat Type**: None


#### Regulatory

**Regulatory Exposure**:
Exempt (hardware provider). Not directly regulated under EU AI Act as GPAI provider or high-risk system. However, Cerebras faces significant US export control and CFIUS scrutiny: the G42 partnership triggered CFIUS national security review due to G42's UAE/China connections, and its TSMC fabrication dependency means it is subject to US-Taiwan semiconductor export regulations. Sam Altman's dual role as OpenAI CEO and Cerebras investor has also drawn governance scrutiny.


### 10. Samsung / SK Hynix

*Source: Samsung_SK_Hynix.json*


#### Identity

**Company Name**: Samsung Electronics / SK Hynix

**Headquarters**: Suwon, South Korea (Samsung) / Icheon, South Korea (SK Hynix)

**Headquarters Region**: Other

**Founded Year**: 1969 (Samsung Electronics) / 1983 (SK Hynix, as Hyundai Electronics)


#### Value Chain

**Role In Value Chain**: hardware

**Value Chain Position**:
Critical component suppliers at the memory layer of the AI hardware stack. Samsung and SK Hynix manufacture High Bandwidth Memory (HBM) chips that are physically stacked onto AI accelerators from NVIDIA, AMD, and custom ASIC designers. They sit between foundries (Samsung also operates its own foundry) and GPU/accelerator makers. Without HBM, modern AI GPUs cannot function -- it is the single most supply-constrained component in the AI value chain. SK Hynix holds ~57% HBM market share, Samsung ~22%, and Micron ~21% (Q3 2025).

**Vertical Integration Level**: Multi-layer


#### Products

**Key Products**:
- HBM3E (12-Hi and 8-Hi stacks): the current generation High Bandwidth Memory used in NVIDIA H200, AMD MI300X, and other AI accelerators. Both companies are planning ~20% price hikes for 2026 due to overwhelming demand. SK Hynix leads in volume and yield; Samsung is catching up.
- HBM4: next-generation memory entering mass production in 2026 for NVIDIA's Rubin platform. SK Hynix targets ~65-70% of NVIDIA's HBM4 supply; Samsung targets 30%+. Offers 11.7 Gbps performance (Samsung) and significant bandwidth improvements over HBM3E.
- Server DRAM (DDR5) and Enterprise SSDs: high-value-added memory products beyond HBM that also benefit from AI data center buildout. Samsung is the world's largest DRAM and NAND manufacturer overall.


#### Business

**Revenue Or Valuation**:
Samsung Electronics FY2025: KRW 333.6T (~$233B) total revenue, KRW 43.6T (~$30.5B) operating profit. DS (semiconductor) division Q4 2025 alone: KRW 44T revenue, KRW 16.4T operating profit (record). Market cap: ~$725B (Feb 2026). SK Hynix FY2025: KRW 97.1T revenue, KRW 47.2T operating profit (record, surpassing Samsung's for the first time). Market cap: ~$396B (Feb 2026). Combined: ~$1.14T market cap.

**Funding Total And Last Round**: Both are publicly traded. Samsung Electronics: KRX 005930. SK Hynix: KRX 000660. SK Hynix is a subsidiary of SK Group (acquired in 2012). No venture funding applicable.

**Business Model**:
Component manufacturing and sales. Both sell memory chips (DRAM, NAND, HBM) to device makers, cloud providers, and OEMs. HBM commands premium pricing (~3-5x conventional DRAM per bit) with ~20% price hikes planned for 2026. Samsung also operates a foundry business (Samsung Foundry) competing with TSMC. Revenue is driven by memory pricing cycles (currently in an AI-driven supercycle), volume shipments, and product mix shift toward high-value HBM and server memory.

**Burn Rate And Runway**:
Not applicable. Both are highly profitable public companies. SK Hynix operating profit: KRW 47.2T in 2025. Samsung semiconductor division operating profit: KRW 43.6T in 2025. Both are increasing capex massively for 2026 (SK Hynix 4x infrastructure investment increase, Samsung 50% capacity expansion).


#### Openness

**Open Vs Closed Spectrum**:
Closed. HBM is manufactured to industry standards (JEDEC specifications for HBM3E/HBM4) but the manufacturing processes, 3D stacking technology (TSV -- Through-Silicon Via), and advanced packaging are proprietary trade secrets. Customers buy finished memory components with standard interfaces.


#### Startup Relevance

**Relevance For Startups**:
Samsung and SK Hynix are invisible but critical to every AI startup's cost structure. Entrepreneurs should understand: (1) HBM supply is the bottleneck that determines GPU availability -- when HBM is scarce, GPU prices rise and cloud compute costs increase for everyone, directly impacting AI startup unit economics; (2) The HBM duopoly (SK Hynix + Samsung control ~80% of market) means memory pricing power is concentrated, and the planned 20% HBM3E price hikes for 2026 will flow through to higher GPU and cloud compute costs; (3) The memory supercycle creates a timing dynamic -- startups launching inference-heavy products in 2026-2027 should budget for potentially higher compute costs as HBM demand continues to outstrip supply; (4) For hardware startups designing custom AI chips (ASICs), HBM allocation is a critical supply chain risk -- securing HBM supply from Samsung or SK Hynix requires significant volume commitments and long lead times; (5) The transition from HBM3E to HBM4 in 2026 will improve AI chip performance, potentially improving inference cost/performance ratios for applications built on next-gen hardware.

**Accessibility**:
Not directly accessible to startups. Samsung and SK Hynix sell HBM to large chip designers (NVIDIA, AMD, Google, custom ASIC companies) who integrate it into accelerators. Startups interact with HBM indirectly through cloud GPU pricing and availability. For hardware startups designing custom silicon, minimum order quantities for HBM are in the millions of units, requiring substantial capital and volume commitments. Standard DRAM and SSDs are available through normal distribution channels.

**Primary Model Dependency**: None. Samsung and SK Hynix are component manufacturers at the lowest layer of the hardware stack, with no dependency on any foundation model.


#### Ecosystem

**Key Partnerships**:
NVIDIA: the primary customer for HBM from both companies. SK Hynix supplies ~65-70% of NVIDIA's HBM needs; Samsung targets 30%+ for HBM4 in 2026. AMD: major HBM customer for MI300X/MI400 accelerators. Google, Microsoft, Amazon: custom ASIC programs requiring HBM. TSMC and Samsung Foundry: advanced packaging partnerships for integrating HBM onto logic chips (CoWoS from TSMC, I-Cube from Samsung). Intel: HBM integration for Gaudi accelerators.

**Competitive Moat**:
Extreme manufacturing complexity: HBM requires stacking 8-12 layers of DRAM dies using Through-Silicon Via (TSV) technology with near-perfect yields, a process only three companies worldwide can execute at scale (Samsung, SK Hynix, Micron). SK Hynix's moat is its 2+ year head start in HBM3/HBM3E mass production and trusted supplier relationship with NVIDIA built on consistent yields. Samsung's moat is its vertical integration (owns both memory fab and logic foundry), enabling co-optimized packaging. Both benefit from massive capital requirements ($10B+ per new fab) creating insurmountable barriers to entry. The 2026 supply is already sold out, demonstrating pricing power.

**Data Moat Type**: None


#### Regulatory

**Regulatory Exposure**:
Exempt (hardware component manufacturer). Not regulated under EU AI Act. However, both face significant geopolitical and trade regulation exposure: (1) US export controls restrict sales of advanced memory to China, affecting both companies' China revenue; (2) South Korean government semiconductor subsidy programs and technology protection laws apply; (3) China's rare earth export controls (targeting chipmaking materials) create supply chain risk for both; (4) Both companies have been subject to antitrust investigations in the past (DRAM price-fixing settlements). Samsung's foundry business faces separate regulatory considerations for advanced node chips.

## Layer 2 — Cloud Infrastructure / Compute


### 11. AWS (Amazon)

*Source: AWS_Amazon.json*


#### Identity

**Company Name**: Amazon Web Services (AWS)

**Headquarters**: Seattle, USA

**Headquarters Region**: US

**Founded Year**: 2006


#### Value Chain

**Role In Value Chain**: cloud

**Value Chain Position**:
Core infrastructure layer of the AI value chain. AWS sits between hardware manufacturers (NVIDIA GPUs, plus its own Trainium/Inferentia custom silicon fabricated by TSMC) and the application/model layers above. It serves AI labs (Anthropic, OpenAI), enterprises, and startups with compute, storage, networking, and managed AI services. AWS also develops its own foundation models (Amazon Nova) and hosts third-party models via Bedrock, making it both a cloud provider and an emerging model hub/API provider.

**Vertical Integration Level**: Multi-layer


#### Products

**Key Products**:
- Amazon Bedrock: fully managed service providing access to foundation models from Anthropic (Claude), Meta (Llama), Mistral, Stability AI, Amazon (Nova), and others via API. Described by Amazon as aiming to be 'as big as EC2' long-term. Multi-billion-dollar annualized run rate business as of Q4 2025.
- Custom AI Silicon (Trainium & Inferentia): Trainium2 chips for AI training (30-40% more price-performant than comparable GPUs), with 1.4M+ chips deployed and Project Rainier cluster (~500K Trainium2 chips for Anthropic). Trainium3 announced at re:Invent 2025 with 40% improvement over Trainium2. Inferentia chips optimized for inference workloads.
- Amazon SageMaker: end-to-end ML platform for building, training, and deploying models. Now includes serverless model customization and unified data/AI governance. Combined with Bedrock, forms AWS's complete AI/ML stack.


#### Business

**Revenue Or Valuation**:
AWS FY2025 revenue: $128.7B (+20% YoY). Q4 2025 quarterly revenue: $35.58B (+24% YoY). Annualized run rate: $142B. AWS operating income Q4 2025: $12.47B (35% operating margin). Amazon parent company market cap: ~$2.25T (Feb 2026).

**Funding Total And Last Round**:
Division of Amazon.com, Inc. (NASDAQ: AMZN), a public company since 1997. No separate funding rounds. Amazon total market cap ~$2.25T (Feb 2026). Amazon plans ~$200B capex in 2026, predominantly for AWS.

**Business Model**:
Usage-based cloud computing (pay-as-you-go for compute, storage, networking, AI services). Revenue streams include: EC2/compute instances, S3 storage, managed AI services (Bedrock per-token pricing, SageMaker), data transfer fees, support contracts, and marketplace commissions. AWS generates the majority of Amazon's operating profit (~$45.6B operating income in FY2025).


#### Openness

**Open Vs Closed Spectrum**:
Hybrid. AWS hosts open-weight models (Llama, Mistral) on Bedrock alongside closed models (Claude, Nova). Leads 1,200+ open-source projects on GitHub. Core cloud services are proprietary. Amazon Nova models are closed. Trainium/Inferentia chips use proprietary Neuron SDK. Historically controversial relationship with open source (forking Elasticsearch into OpenSearch, etc.).


#### Startup Relevance

**Relevance For Startups**:
AWS is the largest cloud platform and the default infrastructure choice for a majority of AI startups. Entrepreneurs should understand: (1) AWS offers the broadest service catalog (200+ services), meaning startups can build almost anything without leaving the ecosystem, but this creates significant switching costs and vendor lock-in; (2) Bedrock provides easy API access to multiple foundation models (Claude, Llama, Mistral, Nova), letting startups compare and switch models without infrastructure changes -- critical for cost optimization; (3) Trainium/Inferentia custom chips offer 30-40% cost savings over NVIDIA GPUs for training and inference, which directly improves unit economics for AI-heavy startups; (4) AWS holds ~29-30% cloud market share with 4.19M+ customers, meaning most enterprise buyers already have AWS accounts -- this reduces friction for B2B AI startups selling to enterprises; (5) The $38B OpenAI deal and $8B Anthropic investment signal that the largest AI labs run on AWS, validating its AI infrastructure; (6) AWS Activate offers up to $100K in credits (up to $300K for foundation model builders), with an additional marketplace of $800K in third-party tool credits; (7) The $200B planned capex for 2026 means AWS is massively expanding capacity, reducing the GPU/compute scarcity that has constrained AI startups.

**Accessibility**:
Very accessible for startups. AWS Activate Founders: $1K credits for self-funded startups. AWS Activate Portfolio: up to $100K credits for VC-backed startups. Generative AI tier: up to $300K for foundation model builders. Free tier includes 12 months of limited EC2, S3, Lambda, and other services. Bedrock pricing is per-token (pay only for what you use). SageMaker offers serverless options. 92% of AWS customers spend under $1K/month. Extensive documentation, tutorials, and community support.

**Primary Model Dependency**:
None as a cloud provider. AWS develops its own Amazon Nova models and hosts third-party models (Claude, Llama, Mistral) via Bedrock. Strategic dependency on Anthropic (Claude) as a key differentiating model on the platform.


#### Ecosystem

**Key Partnerships**:
Anthropic: $8B investment, primary cloud/training partner, Project Rainier (~500K Trainium2 chips), dedicated $11B data center campus. OpenAI: $38B capacity deal signed Nov 2025 to run workloads on AWS GPUs. NVIDIA: major customer for GPUs deployed across AWS regions. TSMC: fabrication partner for Trainium and Graviton chips. Enterprise partners: SAP, Salesforce, Databricks, Snowflake integrated with AWS. Startup ecosystem: 4.19M+ customers, AWS Partner Network with thousands of ISVs and consulting partners.

**Competitive Moat**:
Largest cloud infrastructure (29-30% global market share). Broadest service portfolio (200+ services). Massive installed base (4.19M customers) creates network effects and ecosystem lock-in. Custom silicon (Trainium, Inferentia, Graviton) provides unique price-performance advantages unavailable elsewhere. First-mover advantage in cloud (launched 2006, years ahead of competitors). Bedrock's multi-model approach makes it a one-stop shop for AI model access. $200B planned 2026 capex creates capacity barriers to entry. Deep enterprise relationships and compliance certifications across industries.

**Data Moat Type**: Distribution / Network effects


#### Regulatory

**Regulatory Exposure**:
Exempt as cloud infrastructure provider under EU AI Act (not a GPAI provider for third-party models hosted on Bedrock). However, Amazon Nova models may qualify as GPAI, subjecting them to transparency and documentation obligations from August 2025. Amazon is a signatory of the EU General-Purpose AI Code of Practice. US CLOUD Act creates sovereignty tensions with EU customers (US providers can be compelled to hand over EU-hosted data). AWS offers EU-specific regions and data residency controls but jurisdictional conflict remains a concern for European regulators.


### 12. Google Cloud (GCP)

*Source: Google_Cloud_GCP.json*


#### Identity

**Company Name**: Google Cloud (GCP)

**Headquarters**: Sunnyvale, USA

**Headquarters Region**: US

**Founded Year**: 2008


#### Value Chain

**Role In Value Chain**: cloud

**Value Chain Position**:
Core infrastructure and AI platform layer. Google Cloud sits between its own custom hardware (TPUs designed in-house, fabricated by Broadcom/TSMC) and the application layer above. Uniquely integrated with Google DeepMind, giving it first-party access to Gemini models. Serves AI labs (Anthropic uses TPUs), enterprises, and startups with compute, storage, AI/ML services, and managed model APIs via Vertex AI. Also competes at the model layer through Gemini, making it simultaneously a cloud provider, model creator, and API provider.

**Vertical Integration Level**: Full-stack


#### Products

**Key Products**:
- Vertex AI & Gemini: unified AI development platform with access to Gemini models (Google DeepMind), 200+ open models via Model Garden, and tools for fine-tuning, RAG, agents, and deployment. 4M+ developers building with Gemini, 20x YoY usage growth. Includes Vertex AI Search, Vertex AI Agents, and enterprise-grade AI solutions.
- Cloud TPUs (Tensor Processing Units): custom AI accelerators now in 7th generation (Ironwood, announced 2025). Ironwood offers 5x peak compute and 6x HBM vs prior generation Trillium. TPUs power both Google's own AI (Search, YouTube, Gemini) and external customers (Anthropic's 1GW+ deal). Unique hardware competitive advantage unavailable on other clouds.
- Google Kubernetes Engine (GKE) & Core Cloud Infrastructure: enterprise cloud platform with compute (Compute Engine), storage (Cloud Storage, BigQuery), data analytics (BigQuery, Looker), and Kubernetes orchestration. Google originated Kubernetes and maintains leadership in cloud-native container orchestration.


#### Business

**Revenue Or Valuation**:
Google Cloud FY2025 revenue: ~$58.8B (Q1: $12.3B, Q2: $13.6B, Q3: $15.2B, Q4: $17.7B). Q4 2025 growth: +48% YoY. Annualized run rate exceeding $70B. Q4 operating income: $5.3B (30.1% operating margin, up from 17.5% a year prior). Cloud backlog: $240B (doubled YoY). Alphabet parent company market cap: ~$4T (Feb 2026).

**Funding Total And Last Round**:
Division of Alphabet Inc. (NASDAQ: GOOGL), public company. No separate funding rounds. Alphabet total market cap ~$4T (Feb 2026). Alphabet plans $75B capex in 2025 and up to $185B in 2026, with significant allocation to Google Cloud AI infrastructure.

**Business Model**:
Usage-based cloud computing (pay-as-you-go for compute, storage, AI services). Revenue streams include: Compute Engine instances, Cloud Storage, BigQuery data analytics, Vertex AI (per-token/per-request for Gemini API, training compute hours), TPU compute time, Google Workspace subscriptions (per-user/month), support contracts, and marketplace commissions. Google Cloud turned consistently profitable in 2023 and operating margins have expanded rapidly to 30%+ by Q4 2025.


#### Openness

**Open Vs Closed Spectrum**:
Hybrid. Strong open-source heritage: created Kubernetes, TensorFlow, JAX, Kubeflow, and contributed to many foundational AI/ML tools. Gemma open-weight models released under permissive licenses. Gemini flagship models are closed/API-only. Cloud services are proprietary. TPU hardware is proprietary (only available on GCP). Model Garden hosts 200+ open models. Overall more open-source-friendly than AWS or Azure historically, but core competitive advantages (Gemini, TPUs) remain closed.


#### Startup Relevance

**Relevance For Startups**:
Google Cloud is a compelling choice for AI-native startups due to its unique combination of first-party AI models, custom hardware, and aggressive startup incentives. Entrepreneurs should understand: (1) Vertex AI + Gemini provides a tightly integrated stack from model API to fine-tuning to deployment, reducing the 'glue code' burden that plagues multi-vendor setups; (2) TPUs offer a genuine alternative to NVIDIA GPUs -- Anthropic chose Google TPUs for next-gen Claude training, validating TPU competitiveness for frontier AI; (3) Google Cloud's 48% Q4 growth rate (fastest among hyperscalers) signals momentum and continued investment, meaning the platform will keep improving rapidly; (4) DeepMind integration means Gemini models benefit from Google's $30B+ annual R&D, giving startups building on Gemini access to capabilities funded at a scale no startup could match; (5) The $240B cloud backlog signals massive enterprise demand, validating GCP as enterprise-ready (historically a weakness vs AWS); (6) Google for Startups Cloud Program offers up to $350K in credits for AI startups over 2 years, one of the most generous programs available; (7) BigQuery and data analytics tools are best-in-class for data-intensive AI applications; (8) Kubernetes expertise (Google created K8s) matters for startups planning multi-cloud or hybrid deployments.

**Accessibility**:
Very accessible with the most generous AI startup credits. Google for Startups Cloud Program: up to $200K credits over 2 years (standard), up to $350K for AI startups (Year 1: 100% up to $250K, Year 2: 20% up to $100K). Early-stage unfunded startups: $2K credits. Additional $10K for partner LLM models via Model Garden. $300 free trial credits for all new users. Vertex AI offers per-token Gemini API pricing competitive with OpenAI/Anthropic. TPU access available on-demand and via reserved capacity. Free tier includes limited Compute Engine, Cloud Storage, BigQuery usage. Extensive documentation, Colab integration, and Qwiklabs for learning.

**Primary Model Dependency**:
Self-sufficient. Google Cloud develops Gemini models internally through Google DeepMind. Also hosts third-party models (Claude, Llama, Mistral) via Vertex AI Model Garden and has a strategic partnership with Anthropic ($3B+ invested, TPU supply deal).


#### Ecosystem

**Key Partnerships**:
Anthropic: $3B+ investment (10% equity stake), tens-of-billions-dollar TPU deal (1M+ TPUs, 1GW+ compute capacity for next-gen Claude). NVIDIA: partnership to bring Gemini to NVIDIA Blackwell systems. Hugging Face: native TPU support for all open models on Hugging Face. Apple: partnership for AI integration (chosen over Anthropic and Perplexity). SAP, Salesforce, and major enterprise ISVs integrated with Google Cloud. Dell: partner for on-premises Gemini deployments. Open source community: creator/maintainer of Kubernetes, TensorFlow, JAX.

**Competitive Moat**:
DeepMind integration provides exclusive first-party access to Gemini frontier models, a capability neither AWS nor Azure can replicate (they must partner with external labs). Custom TPU silicon now in 7th generation (Ironwood) -- the only cloud provider besides AWS with competitive custom AI chips, and the only one making them available to external customers at scale. Data analytics leadership (BigQuery) creates stickiness for data-heavy workloads. Kubernetes origin story gives credibility in cloud-native and multi-cloud. Fastest revenue growth among hyperscalers (48% Q4 2025) attracting developer and enterprise attention. $240B backlog provides revenue visibility.

**Data Moat Type**: Distribution / Network effects


#### Regulatory

**Regulatory Exposure**:
GPAI provider for Gemini models under EU AI Act. Google committed publicly to compliance with EU AI Act GPAI provider obligations (effective August 2, 2025), including transparency, technical documentation, and copyright compliance. Early signatory of the EU General-Purpose AI Code of Practice. Google Cloud offers EU data residency and sovereignty controls but faces same US CLOUD Act jurisdictional tension as other US hyperscalers. GDPR compliant: customer data at rest stays in customer's cloud environment, not used for model training without permission. Alphabet's 70%+ share of EU cloud infrastructure (combined with other US providers) is driving EU sovereign cloud initiatives that could benefit European alternatives.


### 13. Microsoft Azure

*Source: Microsoft_Azure.json*


#### Identity

**Company Name**: Microsoft Azure

**Headquarters**: Redmond, USA

**Headquarters Region**: US

**Founded Year**: 2010


#### Value Chain

**Role In Value Chain**: cloud

**Value Chain Position**:
Core cloud infrastructure and AI platform layer. Azure sits between hardware suppliers (NVIDIA GPUs, AMD, plus custom in-house silicon like Maia AI accelerator and Cobalt ARM CPU, fabricated by TSMC) and the application layer above. Uniquely positioned through the exclusive OpenAI partnership: Azure is the sole cloud provider for OpenAI's API products and hosts GPT-4, GPT-4o, o1, and o3 models via Azure OpenAI Service. Also hosts Anthropic Claude, Mistral, Meta Llama, and Cohere models via Azure AI Foundry, making it simultaneously a cloud provider, model hub, and API provider. Serves enterprises, AI labs, and startups with compute, storage, networking, and managed AI/ML services. The $250B committed Azure spend from OpenAI alone cements its position as the primary cloud for frontier AI.

**Vertical Integration Level**: Full-stack


#### Products

**Key Products**:
- Azure OpenAI Service & Azure AI Foundry: managed access to OpenAI models (GPT-4o, o1, o3, DALL-E, Whisper) plus third-party models (Claude, Mistral, Llama, Cohere) through a unified platform.
